</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>Data processing, management and visualization are central and critical components of a state of the art high-throughput mass spectrometry (MS)-based proteomics experiment, and are often some of the most time-consuming steps, especially for labs without much bioinformatics support. The growing interest in the field of proteomics has triggered an increase in the development of new software libraries, including freely available and open-source software. From database search analysis to post-processing of the identification results, even though the objectives of these libraries and packages can vary significantly, they usually share a number of features. Common use cases include the handling of protein and peptide sequences, the parsing of results from various proteomics search engines output files, and the visualization of MS-related information (including mass spectra and chromatograms). In this review, we provide an overview of the existing software libraries, open-source frameworks and also, we give information on some of the freely available applications which make use of them. This article is part of a Special Issue entitled: Computational Proteomics in the Post-Identification Era. Guest Editors: Martin Eisenacher and Christian Stephan.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Abbreviations:</strong> AMT, Accurate Mass Tag; ATAQS, Automated and Targeted Analysis with Quantitative SRM; CV, Controlled Vocabulary; DAO, Data Access Object; EBI, European Bioinformatics Institute; emPAI, exponentially modified Protein Abundance Index; FDR, False Discovery Rate; (HUPO)-PSI, (Human Proteome Organization) — Proteomics Standards Initiative; GUI, Graphical User Interface; ICAT, Isotope-Coded Affinity Tags; ICPL, Isotope-Coded Protein Label; IPTL, Isobaric Peptide Termini Labeling; ISB, Institute for Systems Biology; iTRAQ, Isobaric Tag for Relative and Absolute Quantitation; JPL, Java Proteomic Library; LC-MS, Liquid Chromatography–Mass Spectrometry; LIMS, Laboratory Information Management System; MGF, Mascot Generic Format; MIAPE, Minimum Information About a Proteomics Experiment; MS, Mass Spectrometry; SILAC, Stable Isotope Labeling by Amino acids in Cell culture; PASSEL, PeptideAtlas SRM Experiment Library; PRIDE, PRoteomics IDEntifications (database); PSM, Peptide Spectrum Match; PTM, Post-Translational Modifications; RT, Retention Time; SRM, Selected Reaction Monitoring; TMT, Tandem Mass Tag; TOPP, The OpenMS Proteomics Pipeline; TPP, Trans-Proteomic Pipeline</p></section><section id="kwd-group2" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> Proteomics, Databases, Bioinformatics, Software libraries, Application programming interface, Open source software</p></section></section><section class="abstract" id="abstract2"><h2>Highlights</h2>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:none">
<li id="u0005">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline">A review of existing open-source software for computational proteomics.</p>
</li>
<li id="u0010">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline">Available software for each step in a typical MS experiment is described.</p>
</li>
<li id="u0015">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline">OpenMS, TPP, compomics, ProteoWizard, JPL, PRIDE toolsuite are covered in detail.</p>
</li>
<li id="u0020">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline">Different programming languages are considered (Java, Perl, C++ or Python).</p>
</li>
</ul></section><section id="s0005"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>Mass spectrometry (MS)-based proteomics has become an increasingly prominent field in the last decade, allowing the identification, quantification and characterization of peptides and proteins in biological samples <a href="#bb0005" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0005">[1,2]</a>. Developments of technology and methodology in the field have been rapid over the last years and are providing improved and novel strategies for the global understanding of cellular function. Different strategies for peptide and protein identification are followed by the different experimental approaches available. In the bottom-up approaches, complex protein mixtures are enzymatically digested into potentially very complex peptide mixtures, which are then fractionated by multidimensional chromatography steps before they are subjected to tandem MS <a href="#bb0015" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0015">[3]</a>. Currently, this is the most used strategy. In the top-down approaches <a href="#bb0785" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0785">[4]</a>, intact proteins are measured and different isoforms can be isolated before the MS identification and characterization are performed. This is especially useful to unravel complex patterns of splice variations, or post-translational modifications (PTMs) <a href="#bb0785" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0785">[4]</a>. Finally, the targeted proteomics approaches <a href="#bb0790" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0790">[5]</a> differ fundamentally from the previous two approaches, since the mass spectrometer is here programmed to detect and analyze only pre-selected proteins. The most popular targeted approach is called SRM (Selected Reaction Monitoring). In addition, quantification techniques can measure the differences in protein expression between different physiological states of a biological system. Nowadays, MS-based techniques comprise some of the most used quantitative approaches <a href="#bb0030" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0030">[6]</a>.</p>
<p>The advances in the MS proteomics methods are closely related to the parallel developments that have happened in bioinformatics. Several computational methods can now be used to identify peptides and proteins. The most popular ones are based on the use of search engines <a href="#bb0795" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0795">[7]</a> and protein sequence databases, but there are other approaches such as <em>de novo</em> sequencing (especially used when the genome of the studied organism is not well known) <a href="#bb0040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0040">[8,9]</a> and the spectral library searches <a href="#bb0800" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0800">[10,11]</a>. As a result, there are several well established software applications like Mascot <a href="#bb0060" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0060">[12]</a>, X!Tandem <a href="#bb0065" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0065">[13]</a>, Sequest <a href="#bb0070" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0070">[14]</a>, MyriMatch <a href="#bb0075" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0075">[15]</a>, SpectraST <a href="#bb0055" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0055">[11]</a>, OMSSA <a href="#bb0080" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0080">[16]</a>, and Andromeda <a href="#bb0085" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0085">[17]</a>, among others.</p>
<p>However, there is an increasing demand for high-performance bioinformatics solutions that can help to address the various data processing and data interpretation challenges in the field <a href="#bb0090" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0090">[18–20]</a>. And while these tools can vary substantially, a basic set of features can be shared between many of them. Common MS data processing tasks comprise theoretical analysis of proteomes, processing of raw spectra, file format conversions, generation of identification statistics, and the storage/visualization of raw data, identification and quantitation results. As a consequence, the number of available open-source software libraries and frameworks has increased significantly in recent years. These platforms provide common software infrastructures, features and algorithms that can help in the development of new applications and tools. Previous reviews addressed the advances in the field of software tools and bioinformatics applications for proteomics MS experiments <a href="#bb0090" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0090">[18,19,21–23]</a>, but open-source frameworks and libraries were not evaluated in detail. There are now a wide variety of software solutions covering all aspects of LC–MS/MS data analysis, which are developed and maintained by an active community of bioinformaticians and software developers <a href="#bb0115" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0115">[23]</a>. In this review we focus mostly on open-source frameworks, software libraries and downloadable tools, so most of the existing online resources have not been included. The R programming language will not be considered here either since it is covered in another manuscript in this special issue. We will follow the steps of a typical tandem MS proteomics workflow to describe the available software suitable for each of them. In addition, we will mention some tools that are specific for targeted proteomics approaches (SRM).</p></section><section id="s0015"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Tandem MS proteomics workflow and open-source software</h2>
<p>A typical tandem MS proteomics experiment starts with the isolation of proteins from the sample or samples of interest <a href="#bb0120" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0120">[24–26]</a>. Different approaches are used to reduce the complexity of samples such as the electrophoresis-based <a href="#bb0135" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0135">[27–29]</a> and chromatography-based workflows <a href="#bb0150" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0150">[30–32]</a>. As the peptides are injected into the mass spectrometer, the instrument first acquires a precursor ion scan, wherein each intact peptide ion produces a peak in the mass spectrum. A mass spectrum of the fragment ions, known as a tandem mass (MS/MS) spectrum, is then obtained for each selected precursor. A typical analysis of experimental data coming from a MS/MS study will involve most if not all of the following seven steps (<a href="#f0005" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>): 1) <em>In silico</em> analysis of proteome/sequence databases, 2) conversion of raw data to open data formats, 3) mass spectrum pre-processing, 4) peptide and protein identification, 5) peptide and protein identification post-processing, 6) quantification analysis, and 7) data storage in a LIMS and transfer to public data repositories (<a href="#f0005" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0005"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 1.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=3898926_gr1.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/f903/3898926/ef6abcff0b88/gr1.jpg" loading="lazy" height="428" width="710" alt="Fig. 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f0005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Schema of the possible computational processing steps of a proteomics data set.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Some of the most popular and most extensively used open-source frameworks are OpenMS <a href="#bb0165" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0165">[33]</a>, the Trans-Proteomic Pipeline (TPP) <a href="#bb0170" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0170">[34]</a>, the Computational Omics (Compomics) suite <a href="#bb0175" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0175">[35–39]</a>, the PRoteomics IDEntifications (PRIDE) database toolsuite <a href="#bb0200" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0200">[40,41]</a>, ProteoWizard <a href="#bb0210" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0210">[42]</a> and the Java Proteomic Library (JPL) <a href="#bb0215" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0215">[43,44]</a>. Other well-known libraries/frameworks with a more specialized scope include <em>InsilicoSpectro</em>
<a href="#bb0225" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0225">[45]</a>, <em>multiplierz</em>
<a href="#bb0230" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0230">[46]</a>, <em>mMass</em>
<a href="#bb0235" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0235">[47]</a>, <em>mzMine</em>
<a href="#bb0240" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0240">[48]</a>, <em>msInspect</em>
<a href="#bb0245" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0245">[49]</a>, <em>MSQuant</em> and <em>MASPECTRAS</em>
<a href="#bb0250" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0250">[50]</a>. The aims and functionalities of each framework and library are explored in detail in the following sections.</p>
<section id="s0020"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Highlights of the main open-source libraries and frameworks</h3>
<section id="s0025"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.1. OpenMS</h4>
<p>OpenMS is a software framework for enabling rapid application development in MS. It has been designed to be portable and robust while offering rich functionalities, ranging from the availability of basic data structures to sophisticated algorithms for data analysis. OpenMS (<a href="http://open-ms.sourceforge.net/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://open-ms.sourceforge.net/</a>) is mainly developed in C++ and makes use of several external libraries such as: (i) Qt (<a href="http://qt.nokia.com/products/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://qt.nokia.com/products/</a>), which provides visualization and database support; (ii) Xerces (<a href="http://xerces.apache.org/xerces-c/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://xerces.apache.org/xerces-c/</a>) for XML file parsing; (iii) libSVM (<a href="http://www.csie.ntu.edu.tw/~cjlin/libsvm" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://www.csie.ntu.edu.tw/~cjlin/libsvm</a>), for machine learning algorithms; and (iv) the GNU Scientific Library (GSL, <a href="http://www.gnu.org/software/gsl/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://www.gnu.org/software/gsl/</a>), used for mathematical and statistical analysis. The framework architecture consists of several layers, a core application programming interface (API), which captures the MS data and complementary metadata, and a higher-level functionality API that contains database I/O, file I/O and other analysis algorithms.</p>
<p>The framework contains a complete set of examples to extend and use the libraries. In particular, the package for signal processing provides several filters to reduce the chemical and random noise, as well as the baseline trends in the MS measurements. In addition, the quantitation package allows the analysis of different samples using SILAC, iTRAQ and label-free algorithms <a href="#bb0165" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0165">[33]</a>. Finally, the TOPP (The OpenMS Proteomics Pipeline) and TOPPView tutorials describe in detail the OpenMS tools and the user interface. Also, they provide a complete list and the corresponding command line interfaces of all the TOPP tools contained in each release.</p></section><section id="s0030"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.2. Trans-Proteomic Pipeline</h4>
<p>The Trans-Proteomic Pipeline (TPP, <a href="http://sourceforge.net/projects/sashimi/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://sourceforge.net/projects/sashimi/</a>) <a href="#bb0170" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0170">[34]</a> contains several very popular tools in the field. Developed at the Institute for Systems Biology (ISB, Seattle, USA), the framework comprises a set of components, libraries and tools. They encompass most of the steps involved in a proteomics data analysis workflow in a single, integrated software system, including mass spectrometer output file conversion, protein identification statistical validation, quantification by stable isotope ratios, and support for SRM. To summarize the pipeline, raw mass spectrometer output files are first converted to open XML standard formats. These files are run through one or more search engines such as X!Tandem, Mascot, Sequest, or SpectraST.</p>
<p>PeptideProphet <a href="#bb0255" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0255">[51]</a>, iProphet <a href="#bb0805" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0805">[52]</a>, and ProteinProphet <a href="#bb0265" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0265">[53]</a> can then be used to validate the search engine results and to model correct vs. incorrect peptide-spectrum matches (PSMs) and the protein inference. The quantification analysis tools XPRESS <a href="#bb0270" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0270">[54]</a> or SuperHim <a href="#bb0275" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0275">[55]</a> may then be applied with data that derive from labeled or label-free quantitation approaches. In addition, mProphet and mQuest can automate the analysis of SRM data, and provide probabilistic scoring of targeted peptide identifications and derived quantification <a href="#bb0280" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0280">[56]</a>.</p>
<p>The TPP components have been developed using different programming languages such as C++, Perl and Java. This fact complicates the integration with other pieces of code and the development of new applications using the TPP framework.</p></section><section id="s0035"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.3. Computational Omics (Compomics)</h4>
<p>The Compomics framework is an independent platform and pure Java package with a common core API for all the libraries and tools <a href="#bb0175" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0175">[35]</a>. The platform source code, documentation and tools, along with a complete set of examples are freely available at <a href="http://compomics-utilities.googlecode.com" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://compomics-utilities.googlecode.com</a>. The framework contains a set of parsers for popular search engines output files (Mascot, X!Tandem, OMSSA and Proteome Discoverer (Thermo Scientific)). It also includes a collection of user-friendly tools, including among others: (i) ms_lims <a href="#bb0180" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0180">[36]</a> and DBToolkit <a href="#bb0195" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0195">[39]</a> for storing and performing different <em>in silico</em> analysis of proteomics data; (ii) Peptizer <a href="#bb0190" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0190">[38]</a> for manual validation of MS/MS search results; (iii) Rover <a href="#bb0285" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0285">[57]</a>, for visualizing and validating quantitative proteomics data; (iv) FragmentationAnalyzer <a href="#bb0185" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0185">[37]</a> for analyzing MS/MS fragmentation data; (v) the new PeptideShaker (<a href="http://peptide-shaker.googlecode.com" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://peptide-shaker.googlecode.com</a>), for comprehensive MS data combined analysis of results from multiple search engines (Mascot, OMSSA and X!Tandem); and (vi) SearchGUI <a href="#bb0290" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0290">[58]</a>, which provides a unified GUI (Graphical User Interface) for MS identification using multiple search engines (OMSSA and X!Tandem).</p></section><section id="s0040"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.4. ProteoWizard</h4>
<p>The ProteoWizard framework provides a modular and extensible set of open source, cross-platform tools and libraries <a href="#bb0210" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0210">[42]</a>. This platform enables rapid tool creation and unifies data file access to perform standard proteomics and LC–MS analysis computations. Developed in C++, it is cross-compiled and freely available at <a href="http://proteowizard.sourceforge.net/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://proteowizard.sourceforge.net/</a>. ProteoWizard provides multiple independent libraries, which are grouped together at different levels. The framework includes different tools for data conversion and a core API for parsing different data formats. In addition to the open mzML <a href="#bb0810" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0810">[59]</a>, mzXML <a href="#bb0300" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0300">[60]</a>, mzIdentML <a href="#bb0305" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0305">[61]</a>, and mzData XML formats, a variety of proprietary formats can also be handled. As a result, several frameworks/applications such as TPP and Skyline <a href="#bb0310" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0310">[62,63]</a> extend and use the ProteoWizard core APIs and tools. The C++ source code is designed and optimized for high performance and high throughput analysis, and allows researchers to implement novel algorithms or to complete other <em>ad hoc</em> tasks.</p></section><section id="s0045"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.5. Java Proteomic Library (JPL)</h4>
<p>The Java Proteomic Library (JPL, <a href="http://javaprotlib.sourceforge.net/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://javaprotlib.sourceforge.net/</a>), developed in Java by the Swiss Bioinformatics Group (Geneva, Switzerland) provides a strong chemical-based representation of MS proteomics data. It is composed of several modules and APIs for manipulating peptide or protein sequences, PTMs and mass spectra. It also provides methods for <em>in silico</em> protein digestion and peptide fragmentation, which takes into account various ion types and modifications. Many classes dealing with spectrum processing and filtering, and/or spectrum matching and clustering, are also provided. The availability of core classes that represent modifications, peak annotations and chemical entities in a proteomics context, makes JPL the ideal framework to compute physicochemical properties, such as isoelectric point (<em>pI</em>), retention time (RT) and gravy index. In addition, it also contains several standalone tools for performing protein sequence digestion, creating spectrum and sequence decoy databases <a href="#bb0215" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0215">[43]</a>, and performing open modification spectrum library searches (<em>QuickMod/Liberator</em>) <a href="#bb0220" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0220">[44]</a>. JPL is currently being refactored in order to increase its performance, improve structure of classes and the amount of ‘Unit’ tests available. A new version will be officially released once all this work has been finished. The JPL is well-documented and contains different examples about how to use some of its classes.</p></section><section id="s0050"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.6. PRIDE toolsuite</h4>
<p>The PRIDE database was developed at the European Bioinformatics Institute (EBI), as a repository to store the experimental results from bottom-up MS-based proteomics experiments <a href="#bb0200" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0200">[40]</a>. The PRIDE toolsuite (<a href="http://pride-toolsuite.googlecode.com" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://pride-toolsuite.googlecode.com</a>) constitutes a set of pure Java libraries, tools and packages designed to handle MS proteomics experiments from a vast range of approaches, instruments and analysis platforms. The framework contains a set of components such as: (i) the <em>mzGraph Browser</em> library, for visualizing MS spectra, chromatograms and MS/MS spectrum annotation; (ii) the <em>QualityChart</em> library provides a number of charts for performing a quick quality assessment of the MS experiments; (iii) several APIs for parsing standard data proteomics formats such as mzML, mzIdentML, mzTab and PRIDE XML (the PRIDE internal data format); (iv) the <em>XXIndex</em> library enables the fast indexing of large XML files; (v) the <em>PRIDE Utilities</em> library contains classes with some functionality shared by many of the PRIDE related tools; and (vi) the <em>PRIDE core</em> library (<a href="http://ebi-pride.googlecode.com" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://ebi-pride.googlecode.com</a>), for general data management.</p>
<p>The PRIDE Converter 2 <a href="#bb0320" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0320">[64]</a> and the PRIDE Inspector <a href="#bb0205" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0205">[41]</a> are currently the most popular tools of the framework, and both of them offer a user-friendly GUI. PRIDE Converter 2, recently released, is a new submission tool for converting a large variety of popular MS proteomics formats into PRIDE XML, by guiding the user through a wizard-like process. A command line mode (CLI) mode is also available for converting multiple files at once in batch mode. Its predecessor, the original PRIDE Converter tool <a href="#bb0320" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0320">[64]</a>, is currently being phased out, since the new software has been made available. Finally, PRIDE Inspector is a tool that allows the user to efficiently browse, visualize, and perform an initial assessment of MS proteomics data in the PRIDE XML and mzML <a href="#bb0810" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0810">[59]</a> formats, and also allows direct access to a PRIDE MySQL public database instance. Support for the formats mzIdentML and mzTab is in progress. Finally, the most recent addition to the PRIDE-toolsuite is the <em>PRIDE spectra clustering</em> API (<a href="http://pride-spectra-clustering.googlecode.com" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://pride-spectra-clustering.googlecode.com</a>).</p></section></section><section id="s0055"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. <em>In silico</em> analysis of the proteome and sequence databases</h3>
<p>Proteomics experiments targeting specific proteins need to carefully choose the approaches used in order to maximize the possibility that the proteins of interest are present and can be identified <a href="#bb0115" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0115">[23]</a>. For instance, to perform <em>in silico</em> studies of proteomes and sequence databases can enable the optimization of the experimental settings <a href="#bb0325" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0325">[65]</a>. Also, the study of the identified proteins is crucial to predict the protein and peptide properties needed for performing targeted proteomics experiments such as SRM.</p>
<p>The features needed for analyzing protein sequence databases are fortunately well represented in the existing software libraries. For example proteolytic digestion, property estimation (<em>pI</em>, retention time, hydrophobicity, etc.) and amino acid distribution are some of these common features. Certain properties can then be used to design a targeted proteomics workflow to detect proteins, which are often missed in the typical workflows.</p>
<p>A significant number of theoretical analyses about the relationships between the <em>pI</em> and different protein properties such as length, taxonomy or hydrophobicity have been published <a href="#bb0325" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0325">[65–68]</a>. Also, different <em>in silico</em> analyses of the proteome for performing accurate mass and time (AMT) tag <a href="#bb0345" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0345">[69]</a> approaches, the decoy method studies <a href="#bb0350" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0350">[70]</a>, and the analysis of different isolation methods combined with accurate mass <a href="#bb0355" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0355">[71]</a>, are good examples of theoretical proteomics analysis as well. <a href="#t0005" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> shows a list of software libraries that can be used for the <em>in silico</em> analysis of proteins.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="t0005"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 1.</h4>
<div class="caption p"><p>Different libraries for <em>in silico</em> analysis of proteins. <div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/t0005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><section id="s0060"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2.1. OpenMS</h4>
<p>The OpenMS framework offers functionalities for analyzing both the protein sequence databases and the identification results. It provides different functions for predicting sequence properties (retention time, <em>pI</em>, mass, etc.) and reading protein databases from FASTA files.</p></section><section id="s0065"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2.2. Compomics</h4>
<p>The DBToolkit from the Compomics framework (<a href="http://dbtoolkit.googlecode.com" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://dbtoolkit.googlecode.com</a>) provides a GUI to build sequence databases, after performing different processing steps such as protease digestion, decoy and sequence pattern filtering. In addition, the <em>compomics-utilities</em> library can be used programmatically to read and parse FASTA files, perform <em>in silico</em> digestion, and predict sequence properties.</p></section><section id="s0070"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2.3. Java Proteomic Library (JPL)</h4>
<p>The JPL provides different functions for predicting the <em>pI</em> of peptides and proteins with several experimental settings. It also provides: (i) the <em>MassCalc</em> tool to compute masses for proteins or molecules; (ii) <em>ProteinDigester</em> to perform digestion of proteins (Supplementary Information), compute the <em>pI</em> and molecular weight of all the digested peptides; and (iii) <em>Dig2Mz</em> to perform protein digestion and compute the <em>m/z</em> values of all digested peptides that passed the charge filters.</p></section><section id="s0075"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2.4. Other tools, packages and open-source frameworks</h4>
<p><em>InsilicoSpectro</em>
<a href="#bb0225" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0225">[45]</a> was developed in Perl and offers different sets of functionalities, for instance protein digestion, sequence database file readers, property estimation (<em>pI</em>, retention time, mass) and MS fragmentation prediction. Different groups have used extensively this library <a href="#bb0340" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0340">[68,71–73]</a> due the availability of several database file readers, and the possibility to predict different physicochemical properties in heterogeneous experimental settings. <em>Database on Demand</em> (<a href="http://www.ebi.ac.uk/pride/dod/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://www.ebi.ac.uk/pride/dod/</a>) <a href="#bb0370" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0370">[74]</a> is a Java web application that can be used to design customized search databases that provide detailed control over the search space.</p>
<p>Python is not an extensively used programming language in computational proteomics, but in recent years is gaining popularity. Then, <em>Multiplierz</em>
<a href="#bb0230" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0230">[46]</a> and <em>Pyteomics</em> (<a href="http://pypi.python.org/pypi/pyteomics/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://pypi.python.org/pypi/pyteomics/</a>) are frameworks to support proteomics data analytic tasks in this language. Access to the available functionality is provided <em>via</em> high-level Python scripts. Already mentioned features such as the availability of sequence database file readers and the prediction of different physicochemical properties (<em>pI</em>, retention time, mass) are present in both libraries <a href="#bb0230" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0230">[46,75,76]</a>. <em>Pyteomics</em> is fully integrated and currently indexed in the Python Package Repository (PyPI).</p></section></section><section id="s0080"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. MS file parsers and conversion</h3>
<section id="s0085"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.1. Mass spectrometry file formats</h4>
<p>The primary data content produced in the context of a MS-based proteomics experiment are the mass spectra. Each mass spectrometer vendor uses different proprietary file formats to store the spectra produced <a href="#bb0155" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0155">[31,77]</a>. The structure of the data varies depending on the instrument and the experiment type, and the files typically consist of MS1 spectra interleaved with multiple MS/MS spectra. The “aging” issue (as time passes, support for certain formats tends to disappear) and the “binary” character of the files (proprietary software dependency) are two of the main limitations of these file formats. This led to the creation of different XML-based open standard formats <a href="#bb0390" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0390">[78]</a>, since it is impractical for software tools developed for general use to support all these different formats. Since then, the development of such formats has enabled a significant increase in MS data sharing <a href="#bb0200" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0200">[40]</a> and validation <a href="#bb0395" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0395">[79]</a>.</p>
<p><a href="#f0010" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>A shows the evolution of different MS file formats in recent years. For instance, mzXML <a href="#bb0300" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0300">[60]</a>, developed by ISB, was one of the first initiatives quickly adopted by the community. In recent years, the HUPO Proteomics Standards Initiative (PSI) has developed a set of important community XML file formats such as mzML (for MS data) <a href="#bb0810" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0810">[59]</a>, mzIdentML (for peptide/protein identifications) <a href="#bb0305" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0305">[61]</a>, and gelML (for gel data) <a href="#bb0400" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0400">[80]</a>, for proteomics data storing, representation and visualization. Recently, TraML <a href="#bb0820" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0820">[81]</a> has been developed as a standard format for encoding transition lists and associated metadata. Quantitative data can be encoded in the nascent formats mzQuantML (XML-based, <a href="http://mzquantml.googlecode.com" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://mzquantml.googlecode.com</a>) and a text-based tab-delimited file called mzTab (<a href="http://mztab.googlecode.com" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://mztab.googlecode.com</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0010"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 2.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=3898926_gr2.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/f903/3898926/21de3b7fb862/gr2.jpg" loading="lazy" height="424" width="710" alt="Fig. 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f0010/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>(A) Evolution of Mass Spectrometry file formats. (B) Schema of the PRIDE toolsuite tools PRIDE Converter 2 and PRIDE Inspector.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="s0090"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.2. ProteoWizard</h4>
<p>The main tools included in ProteoWizard are: (i) <em>msConvert,</em> for data conversion from vendor proprietary formats to mzML and mzXML; (ii) <em>msDiff,</em> to compare two data files; and (iii) <em>msAccess</em>, providing command line access to MS data files (such as mzML, Supplementary Information).</p>
<p>The <em>msConvert</em> tool is a very popular application that can convert MS data in several proprietary formats such as .WIFF (ABI/Sciex), .BAF (Bruker), .RAW (ThermoFisher Scientific), .D (Agilent) and others, into a mzML, mzXML, mz5 <a href="#bb0825" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0825">[82]</a> (a reimplementation of mzML, based on the efficient, industrial storage backend HDF5, <a href="http://www.hdfgroup.org/HDF5" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://www.hdfgroup.org/HDF5</a>), and the text based formats MGF (Mascot Generic Format) and ms2. Annotation in the mzML files is encoded using ‘CVParam’ elements, which refer to the terms present in a given controlled vocabulary (CV) or ontology. ProteoWizard parses the CV/ontology file at compile time and generates C++ code, which allows convenient, typesafe handling of the CV terms.</p></section><section id="s0095"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.3. PRIDE toolsuite</h4>
<p>PRIDE Converter 2 supports the conversion from different popular data formats into PRIDE XML. The PRIDE Converter 2 framework, as part of PRIDE toolsuite, consists of four different components: <em>PRIDE Converter 2</em>, <em>PRIDE mzTab Generator</em> (to generate mzTab files), <em>PRIDE XML Merger</em> (to merge results from different PRIDE XML files into a single file) and <em>PRIDE XML Filter</em> (to filter out some of the data present in the files). At present, the PRIDE Converter 2 supports several file formats: Mascot, X!Tandem, OMSSA, mzIdentML, SpectraST <a href="#bb0415" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0415">[83]</a>, CRUX <a href="#bb0420" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0420">[84]</a>, MSGF <a href="#bb0425" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0425">[85]</a>, Proteome Discoverer, mzML, dta, MGF, mzData, mzXML, and pkl. New file formats can be supported simply by implementing the Java <em>DAO</em> (Data Access Object) interface. <a href="#f0010" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>B shows how the PRIDE toolsuite tools (PRIDE Converter 2 and PRIDE Inspector) can be combined.</p>
<p>As mentioned before, the PRIDE toolsuite also contains several Java APIs for read/write several standard formats, some of which are used in PRIDE Converter 2 and PRIDE Inspector, but also in other external software: <em>jmzML</em>
<a href="#bb0430" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0430">[86]</a> for mzML, <em>jmzIdentML</em>
<a href="#bb0435" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0435">[87]</a> for mzIdentML, and the new <em>jmzTab</em> (<a href="http://mztab.googlecode.com" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://mztab.googlecode.com</a>) to read and write mzTab files. Both <em>jmzML</em> and <em>jmzIdentML</em> use the <em>XXindex</em> library (<a href="http://pride-toolsuite.googlecode.com" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://pride-toolsuite.googlecode.com</a>), an indexing system for large XML files retrieving, allowing a random access to the data.</p>
<p>Recently, the <em>jmzReader</em>
<a href="#bb0440" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0440">[88]</a> library was developed providing a common programming interface for different XML based and/or peak list formats such as: MGF, ms2, dta, mzData, mzXML, pkl, and mzML. This Java library provides functions to randomly access spectra within the files without the need to load the whole file into memory, and allows easy integration with mzIdentML.</p></section><section id="s0100"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.4. Compomics</h4>
<p>The Compomics framework provides different Java-based parsers for well-known search engines: <em>MascotDatfile</em>
<a href="#bb0445" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0445">[89]</a>, <em>OMSSA Parser</em>
<a href="#bb0450" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0450">[90]</a>, <em>XTandem Parser</em>
<a href="#bb0455" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0455">[91]</a>, and <em>Thermo-MSF-Parser</em>
<a href="#bb0460" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0460">[92]</a>, for Proteome Discoverer. It also provides the <em>jTraML</em>
<a href="#bb0465" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0465">[93]</a> library for the TraML standard file format. Also, <em>PeptideShaker</em> supports the creation of a well-annotated PRIDE XML file from the combined search result files from Mascot, OMSSA and X!Tandem.</p></section><section id="s0105"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.5. Other packages and open-source frameworks</h4>
<p><a href="#t0010" class="usa-link">Table 2</a> shows different libraries that can be used to read and write MS files formats (both peak list and peptide/protein identification files). The JRAP (<a href="http://sashimi.sourceforge.net/software_glossolalia.html#JRAP" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://sashimi.sourceforge.net/software_glossolalia.html#JRAP</a>) library was written in Java at the ISB, and has been historically the most extensively used library to handle mzXML files.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/t0010/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>Other well-focused libraries are <em>MGFp</em>
<a href="#bb0470" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0470">[94]</a>, <em>pyMzML</em>
<a href="#bb0475" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0475">[95]</a>, which enable read/write operations in MGF and mzML files, respectively. In addition, the OpenMS and TPP frameworks support mzML, mzXML, MGF and output files from the search engines Mascot, Sequest and X!Tandem.</p></section></section><section id="s0110"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Mass spectrum pre-processing</h3>
<p>Mass spectrum preprocessing algorithms can increase the number of identified peptides and improve the reliability of the peptide identifications. Five types of pre-processing methods are widely used: spectrum normalization, spectrum clustering, precursor charge determination, spectrum de-noising, and spectrum quality assessment <a href="#bb0480" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0480">[96]</a>. It is worth noticing that these algorithms are also applicable to MS-based metabolomics approaches, which are also being increasingly applied to characterize biological systems.</p>
<p>The basic aim of the data pre-processing steps is to transform the raw MS data files into a file format that facilitates an easy access to the characteristics of each observed ion. These characteristics include <em>m/z</em> values, retention time and ion intensity measurements present in the original raw data files. In addition to these basic features, data preprocessing can extract additional information like the isotope distribution of the ions. <a href="#t0015" class="usa-link">Table 3</a> shows some of the most useful libraries for MS proteomics and metabolomics data preprocessing.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="t0015"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 3.</h4>
